Chinese Economic & Financial Cases — 384 Lines
001 Line H01 Qian Hexagram 01 · Line 1 · ITRI and RCA Technology Transfer — Build Capability Before Acting▶

初九｜Hexagram 01 · Line 1 · ITRI and RCA Technology Transfer — Build Capability Before Acting

001 case_id: XFIN-H01-L1 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

ITRI signed a technology-transfer agreement with RCA and sent staff for training. The lesson here is to develop capacity first. The Lesser Image says that the hidden dragon should not act because yang remains below. Learning processes before industrial expansion illustrates preparation while ability is still immature. Which process should first test the procedures brought back from America? Documents and training do not automatically create stable mass production. This is a retrospective comparison, not evidence that participants decided by consulting the line. The event dates to 1976; later results should be checked against RCA and ITRI records.

When Taiwan planned an integrated-circuit industry, buying equipment alone could not make personnel immediately proficient in manufacturing. Engineers had to learn machinery operation, process control and management in real factories before they could establish production capacity at home. The 1976 collaboration arose from this need.

ITRI signed an integrated-circuit technology-transfer agreement with the American company RCA and sent personnel for training. The transfer involved more than equipment purchases: engineers also had to learn processes and management methods.

Training in the United States did not finish the task. Back in Taiwan, engineers had to adapt what they had observed to different machinery, people and supply conditions. The contract, training and subsequent trial production were connected but distinct stages. Institutional accounts identify these milestones, not each engineer's courses or daily performance.

The sequence runs from the need for technology through agreement and learning to independent local operation. Stable production still had to be demonstrated by later trials, not assumed on the contract date. The file cites RCA and ITRI on the agreement and U.S. training in 1976, a technology-investment case; these references do not provide complete accounts for every participant.

002 Line H01 Qian Hexagram 01 · Line 2 · Emperor Wen Abolishes the Land Tax — Extend Benefits to Producers▶

九二｜Hexagram 01 · Line 2 · Emperor Wen Abolishes the Land Tax — Extend Benefits to Producers

002 case_id: XFIN-H01-L2 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

After Emperor Wen abolished land tax, affected farmers paid less while the state surrendered revenue. The benefit reaching producers offers an analogy to virtue extending to others, although landholders and landless people must be distinguished. The Lesser Image says that the dragon appears in the field and virtue spreads widely. Abolishing one tax did not eliminate all burdens. How much harvest did cultivators retain, and what other levies remained? This is a later comparison, not an actual decision based on the line.

In Han China, harvests sustained farming households while land taxes provided state revenue. The tax rate affected the grain and usable income left after payment. Emperor Wen's court had to balance relief for cultivators with the costs of government.

The Book of Han records that Emperor Wen abolished the land tax, reducing part of the burden on agricultural producers. This involved a trade-off between public revenue and farmers' livelihoods.

Affected households paid less that period, while the state relinquished potential income. The measure did not grant land to all farmers and did not eliminate other taxes or labor obligations. The historical record gives the policy, not household-level tax savings or accounts of how the retained grain was used.

The verifiable sequence is the tax burden, the court's rule change and adjustments to household and state finances. Abolition of one tax must not be presented as the end of all hardship. The file cites HS24A, Emperor Wen's thirteenth-year abolition of land tax from 167 BCE, as a taxation-and-livelihood case, not a complete ledger for every participant.

003 Line H01 Qian Hexagram 01 · Line 3 · ITRI Demonstration Plant and Repeated Practice — Practice Until Knowledge Works▶

九三｜Hexagram 01 · Line 3 · ITRI Demonstration Plant and Repeated Practice — Practice Until Knowledge Works

003 case_id: XFIN-H01-L3 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Training came first, then trial production, and finally preparation for commercial enterprise. The lesson is that repeated practice makes knowledge usable. The Lesser Image describes continuous diligence and returning repeatedly to the right course. The demonstration plant required local teams to operate equipment again and again. It converted training into practice; one successful attempt was not enough forever. How was experience from pilot runs passed to the next group of engineers? This is a retrospective analogy, not a historical use of the line.

After ITRI acquired integrated-circuit technology, documents and training still had to pass the test of actual manufacture. If engineers could not apply procedures to local equipment and materials, technology transfer would not become industrial capacity. A demonstration plant would move learning into production.

Following the transfer and training, ITRI built an integrated-circuit demonstration plant to turn knowledge into manufacturing experience. Trial production helped the team gradually master processes and prepare for industrialization.

At the 1977 plant, personnel learned through trial runs, adjusting equipment and responsibilities when problems arose. Moving from individual training to coordinated manufacture required time, money and a reusable production line. Institutional histories connect the plant to industrialization but do not itemize the cost of every experiment.

Training preceded trial production, which in turn prepared people and technology for business operations. The plant should not be equated with the later performance of every semiconductor company; it was a place where learning was tested and accumulated. The file cites RCA on the plant and trials in 1977, a case of technological accumulation, not complete personal accounts.

004 Line H01 Qian Hexagram 01 · Line 4 · UMC Spin-off from ITRI — Commercialize When Conditions Are Ready▶

九四｜Hexagram 01 · Line 4 · UMC Spin-off from ITRI — Commercialize When Conditions Are Ready

004 case_id: XFIN-H01-L4 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Once Taiwan's integrated-circuit technology had accumulated, a business organization was needed to accept orders. The lesson is to commercialize when conditions permit. The Lesser Image says that advancing at the right time is without blame. UMC's founding illustrates movement from preparation to business operations, which required both competence and organizational support. Who then became responsible for quality, delivery and finance? The market test began after incorporation. This is a later analogy, not proof of divinatory decision-making.

As Taiwan accumulated integrated-circuit expertise, ITRI's research needed a company able to accept market orders. A research unit could test processes without necessarily being equipped for long-term responsibility for quality, customer deadlines and cash flow. Commercialization meant more than renaming a factory.

In 1980, United Microelectronics Corporation (UMC) was spun off from ITRI, transferring technology, people and manufacturing capacity toward business operations. Research results now had to meet product and market demands.

After formation, UMC still faced product development and real customer demand; reliable research origins did not guarantee operating results. Institutional records document incorporation but not the profitability of every product.

The sequence moves from technological accumulation through company formation to independent operations. Sustaining orders and improving processes were tasks after founding, not accomplishments completed on the first day. The file cites ITRI's account of the 1980 UMC spin-off as an industrialization case, not complete accounts for each participant.

005 Line H01 Qian Hexagram 01 · Line 5 · TSMC Establishes the Foundry Model — Integrate Resources Through Leadership▶

九五｜Hexagram 01 · Line 5 · TSMC Establishes the Foundry Model — Integrate Resources Through Leadership

005 case_id: XFIN-H01-L5 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

TSMC was founded in 1987 to manufacture chips for customers under a dedicated foundry model. This gives substance to leadership that integrates resources: equipment, talent and demand must become an operating enterprise, not merely a title. The Lesser Image speaks of the flying dragon in heaven and the great person acting. Manufacturing ability still separated incorporation from repeat orders. How could a factory demonstrate that customers' designs would be delivered to specification? The comparison is retrospective, not evidence that the founders used the line.

Integrated-circuit manufacturing equipment is expensive, and many design companies do not own fabrication plants. Taiwan had an electronics industry and trained talent, but combining technology, personnel and machinery into a company manufacturing for others required a clear business model and committed resources.

Founded in 1987, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC) focused on making chips for customers and established the specialist foundry model. Its economic significance was the combination of technology, equipment and demand into a continuing enterprise.

Customers supplied designs, while the factory had to deliver to specifications and maintain quality and process capability. After founding, TSMC still had to win orders and invest in equipment; its later scale must not be projected backward onto its inception. Corporate histories confirm the founding and model but not the progress of every order.

The story moves from industrial conditions to incorporation and then to customer service and continuous production. The initial decision was not the result: subsequent operations sustained the model. The file cites TSMC's account of its 1987 founding and specialist foundry model, not complete accounts for every participant.

006 Line H01 Qian Hexagram 01 · Line 6 · Emperor Wu’s Wars and Fiscal Expansion — Expansion Has Limits▶

上九｜Hexagram 01 · Line 6 · Emperor Wu’s Wars and Fiscal Expansion — Expansion Has Limits

006 case_id: XFIN-H01-L6 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The Book of Han records both the court's fiscal mobilization and burdens on merchants and residents under Emperor Wu. The Lesser Image warns that the overreaching dragon regrets and fullness cannot last. Military expenditure has sources of funds but also people who bear the cost. Wartime fiscal pressure illustrates the limits of expansion; tax-raising capacity is not inexhaustible resources. Whose production and trade costs rose as the court raised revenue? This is a retrospective comparison. Outcomes require checking HS24B.

During Emperor Wu of Han's reign, prolonged military campaigns required new funds for army supplies and administration. The court used taxation, monopolies and material allocations to finance war and government. Concentrating resources in state hands also affected private trade, property and production.

Continued campaigns increased fiscal needs and prompted revenue-raising and monopoly measures. The Book of Han records both expanded state capacity and pressures borne by ordinary people.

The source describes the mobilization of finance alongside burdens on merchants and residents. Revenue met immediate costs, but excessive levies could alter later production and exchange. Different policies cannot be combined into one aggregate account, and the source does not record each household's gains and losses.

Military spending rose, prompted new fiscal measures and shifted burdens throughout society. This sequence identifies needs, policies and reactions without inventing identical outcomes for every measure. The file cites HS24B on military expenditure, fiscal measures and popular hardship under Emperor Wu, a wartime-finance case rather than complete individual accounts.

007 Line H02 Kun Hexagram 02 · Line 1 · Silver Outflows and Financial Stress — Recognize Early Warning Signs▶

初九｜Hexagram 02 · Line 1 · Silver Outflows and Financial Stress — Recognize Early Warning Signs

007 case_id: XFIN-H02-L1 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The Bank of China and other institutions coordinated responses to silver outflows, but international prices and domestic monetary arrangements could not be fixed by changing one shop's books. The Lesser Image moves from frost underfoot to hard ice as small beginnings accumulate. Silver outflows gradually pressured liquidity, illustrating how modest changes become systemic stress. Could local banks maintain ordinary payments as international silver prices shifted? Cross-border flows could not be solved by one trader. This is a later comparison, not an actual use of the line.

In 1930s China, currency and banking remained closely tied to silver. International silver prices and foreign policies changed external demand and made domestic silver outflows a problem. Banks and merchants using silver for payments and lending faced funding pressure.

Changes in the international silver market brought outflows and financial stress. The Bank of China and other institutions attempted coordinated responses, but the gap between international markets and domestic arrangements was difficult to bridge.

Outflows involved cross-border prices and the national monetary system; adjusting one shop's accounts could not stop them. Later monetary reform changed the relationship between silver and legal-tender currency. Bank histories describe responses but do not establish identical shortages in every city.

The episode ran from international price changes to domestic stress, institutional coordination and systemic adjustment, not all in one day. Actual silver prices, outflow volumes and each bank's gains or losses require separate statistical verification. The file cites SILVER and TWO2 on the 1933–1935 fluctuations and responses, a monetary-crisis case, not complete personal accounts.

008 Line H02 Kun Hexagram 02 · Line 2 · Dujiangyan Irrigation and Long-Term Maintenance — Reliable Supply Requires Solid Foundations▶

九二｜Hexagram 02 · Line 2 · Dujiangyan Irrigation and Long-Term Maintenance — Reliable Supply Requires Solid Foundations

008 case_id: XFIN-H02-L2 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The need for water led to a distribution system maintained over generations. Stable supply rests on a durable foundation. Dujiangyan supported cultivation through continuing water management, illustrating the foundations that sustain production; nature still required engineering oversight. The Lesser Image speaks of straightness, order and the brightness of the earth's way. The life of waterworks depends on long-term maintenance. Who dredged channels and repaired them after construction? This is a retrospective analogy, not a decision based on the line.

Cultivation on the Chengdu Plain depends on seasonal water, while flow and sediment affect the continued use of fields. Dujiangyan uses the terrain to distribute water and combines irrigation with sediment removal. Its builders had to plan how water would enter farmland.

The system uses topography and river currents to divide water, remove sediment and irrigate the Chengdu Plain over a long period. Its economic value depends on agricultural water supplies and continued maintenance.

Once channels were in use, farmers could plan crops, but managers still had to clear, repair and adjust the water distribution. Construction did not end irrigation work: river changes and maintenance affected supply. World Heritage materials describe the system's functions, not each household's annual harvest.

From the need for water through construction to maintenance across generations, the works provided enduring public conditions. Not all agriculture in the region can be credited to one project, and maintenance workers must not be ignored. The file cites DU's heritage introduction and irrigation-system description from the Warring States period, a public-works case, not complete accounts for participants.

010 Line H02 Kun Hexagram 02 · Line 4 · Hong Kong’s Market Intervention and Exit — Know When to Wind Down▶

九四｜Hexagram 02 · Line 4 · Hong Kong’s Market Intervention and Exit — Know When to Wind Down

010 case_id: XFIN-H02-L4 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

After Hong Kong purchased shares in 1998, it launched the Tracker Fund in 1999 to dispose of some holdings gradually. This illustrates knowing when to draw an intervention to a close. The Lesser Image says that a closed bag avoids blame through caution. The fund offers an analogy for reducing exposure carefully, not an argument for withholding communication. When should public holdings be sold, and who could verify the exit rules? Crisis purchases created responsibilities afterward. This is a retrospective comparison, not a decision made through the line.

During the 1998 Asian financial crisis, Hong Kong's government bought shares in the market. After intervening under pressure, it had to manage its holdings. Once the immediate crisis eased, reducing public share ownership became another issue.

After the 1998 purchases, Hong Kong launched the Tracker Fund in 1999 as a way to dispose gradually of part of the holdings. Assets acquired during the crisis thus received a management and exit arrangement.

The 1999 fund allowed some holdings to pass gradually to market investors. Moving from direct ownership to an exit required attention to asset disposal and orderly trading. The fund's launch does not mean all shares were sold that day. Official reports confirm policy milestones, not every investor's profit or loss.

The sequence was crisis purchases, management of holdings and design of a gradual exit. The rationale for intervention and arrangements for withdrawal were different stages of one public decision. The file cites TRACK's reserves-management account of the November 1999 fund launch, a public-asset disposal case, not complete accounts for participants.

011 Line H02 Kun Hexagram 02 · Line 5 · Fan Zhongyan’s Charitable Fields — Inner Responsibility Matters More Than Display▶

九五｜Hexagram 02 · Line 5 · Fan Zhongyan’s Charitable Fields — Inner Responsibility Matters More Than Display

011 case_id: XFIN-H02-L5 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Qian Gongfu recounts that Fan Zhongyan purchased charitable land and used rents for his clan's needs. Property devoted to family support illustrates an inner sense of responsibility; it does not mean everyone must donate all assets. The Lesser Image says that auspicious yellow lower garments signify refinement within. Both the source of assistance and its distribution rules matter. If rents fell short of food, clothing and family ceremonies, how would promises be adjusted? This is a later comparison, not historical divination. The account concerns the mid-eleventh century and should be checked against YI.

Fan Zhongyan of the Northern Song wished to support his clan with continuing income rather than give money only when an urgent need arose. Land could yield rents, but providing food, clothing, marriage and funeral assistance required purchasing property and setting rules for management and eligibility.

Qian Gongfu recounts that Fan bought charitable fields (yitian) and used the rental income to help clan members with daily needs and family rites. The property became not merely a personal holding but an enduring source of relief.

Yields and clan needs could change each year, so the arrangement required supervision to continue. The account praises Fan and does not supply complete annual receipts and payments for every beneficiary.

Buying land, collecting rents and distributing support by rules formed a lasting relief arrangement. Beneficiaries belonged to a particular clan; this should not be equated directly with modern universal welfare. The file cites YI's account of the purchase and daily or monthly payments, in the mid-eleventh century, as clan philanthropy and asset management, not a full ledger.

012 Line H02 Kun Hexagram 02 · Line 6 · Gold Yuan Notes and Depreciation — Conflict Can Exhaust Confidence▶

上九｜Hexagram 02 · Line 6 · Gold Yuan Notes and Depreciation — Conflict Can Exhaust Confidence

012 case_id: XFIN-H02-L6 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The old currency weakened, new Gold Yuan notes were issued, and the replacement also failed to hold value. The lesson is that conflict can exhaust credibility. The Lesser Image speaks of dragons fighting in the field when their way is exhausted. Purchasing power is tested repeatedly in exchange. Rapid depreciation during civil war illustrates a deadlock, but harmed noteholders should not be blamed for it. What could residents buy with the notes the day after issue? This is a later comparison, not an actual use of the line. The episode dates to 1948–1949 and requires checking GOLD.

In 1948, amid civil war and depreciation of the old currency, the Nationalist government issued Gold Yuan notes to restore a medium of exchange. People had to handle conversion of savings and daily payments, while merchants repriced goods in the new currency.

The notes failed to halt depreciation and ceased circulating in 1949. Replacing the currency did not remove the pressures of civil war, and holders continued losing purchasing power.

Wartime fiscal and supply pressures persisted after issuance; the notes depreciated rapidly. By their withdrawal in 1949, people who had exchanged assets for them faced further losses. Collection descriptions confirm issuance and withdrawal dates, not monthly prices for all goods.

The sequence runs from instability of the old currency through the new issue to its failure to retain value. A new name and printed denomination could not repair production, public finance and expectations by proclamation alone. The file cites GOLD on issuance, rapid depreciation and withdrawal in 1948–1949, a financial-crisis case rather than complete individual accounts.

013 Line H03 Zhun Hexagram 03 · Line 1 · Rishengchang’s Intercity Remittances — Build Service Relationships First▶

初九｜Hexagram 03 · Line 1 · Rishengchang’s Intercity Remittances — Build Service Relationships First

013 case_id: XFIN-H03-L1 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Cross-regional payments created demand for Rishengchang's bills and branches, whose reliability had to be built through verification and settlement. The lesson is to establish dependable service relationships at the outset. The Lesser Image speaks of keeping the right intention despite hesitation and winning people by treating them with respect. The draft network illustrates maintaining direction and earning trust. How could a distant branch match a bill to its account? Remittances depended on staff and checks. This is a retrospective analogy, not historical use of the line.

In Qing China, merchants traveling between cities faced the cost and danger of carrying silver themselves. Rishengchang's remittance service required customers who deposited money in one city to trust that a distant branch would honor the draft.

Rishengchang developed remittance through bills and branches in different places. A draft accepted far away required staff, authentication methods and internal rules to work together.

Branches used accounts and anti-forgery checks to validate drafts so payment and receipt could take place in different locations. As branches multiplied, personnel needed consistent recording and verification procedures. Institutional accounts describe the system without preserving every draft's amount and holder.

Cross-regional payment created the demand; bills and cooperating branches supplied the answer. Reliability developed through each verification and settlement, while expansion increased management requirements. The file cites PIAO on the draft house's origins and anti-forgery arrangements from the 1820s, a financial-services case, not complete transaction ledgers.

014 Line H03 Zhun Hexagram 03 · Line 2 · Beijing Notes and Staged Settlement — Recovery Takes Time▶

九二｜Hexagram 03 · Line 2 · Beijing Notes and Staged Settlement — Recovery Takes Time

014 case_id: XFIN-H03-L2 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

After redemption stopped in 1916, Beijing-note holders still needed a solution. Recovery took time: staged settlements over several years illustrate a return to functioning after obstruction, but the line's 'ten years' must not be treated as a fixed financial cycle. The Lesser Image describes difficulty from being obstructed and eventual return to normality. Restoring credit required continued fulfillment. When could holders actually obtain usable assets after the plan was announced? This is a later analogy, not a decision based on the line.

After note redemption was suspended in 1916, holders of Beijing notes still had to deal with the currency they possessed. When banks could not pay as promised, another explanation of policy could not instantly restore confidence.

The problem did not disappear after the suspension. The Bank of China's institutional history describes successive settlements using government bonds, deposit certificates and other arrangements to deal gradually with the obligations.

Staged settlement meant holders continued waiting while the bank explained payment priorities and available resources. The records identify the principal measures but cannot show that every holder received the same outcome at the same time.

The episode began with liabilities left by suspended redemption and continued through years of staged settlement, not one announcement. Recovery of confidence depended on carrying out each stage's promises. The file cites JING on Beijing-note settlements from 1916 to 1923, a monetary-credit recovery case rather than complete accounts for each holder.

015 Line H03 Zhun Hexagram 03 · Line 3 · Wang Mang’s Currency Changes — Do Not Advance Without Information▶

九三｜Hexagram 03 · Line 3 · Wang Mang’s Currency Changes — Do Not Advance Without Information

015 case_id: XFIN-H03-L3 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Wang Mang repeatedly changed currencies and introduced different forms and denominations. The lesson is not to rush ahead without adequate information. The Lesser Image describes pursuing a deer without a guide and advises the wise person to stop. Official face values still had to work in real transactions. Confusing coinage is a counterexample to acting without practical guidance; administrative will cannot replace market understanding. How would buyer and seller calculate one price amid repeated changes? This is a retrospective analogy, not historical use of the line.

Wang Mang repeatedly changed the monetary system and introduced coins of different shapes and denominations. Everyday trade needed buyers and sellers to accept a common payment unit. Repeated changes between old and new currencies forced merchants and residents to reassess values and recalculate prices.

The Book of Han records problems in private exchange and violations of currency rules under Wang Mang's multiple coinage reforms. These show a gap between administrative orders and everyday practice.

After new rules were proclaimed, money still had to be available and conversion rates intelligible; otherwise traders might avoid using it. Sources do not report every household's gain or loss, so individual experiences cannot be generalized.

Repeated policy changes preceded problems of conversion and compliance. If a shared monetary measure is unstable, the rules themselves become a transaction cost. The file cites HS24B on Wang Mang's coinage and baohuo system in the early first century CE, a monetary-reform case rather than full personal ledgers.

017 Line H03 Zhun Hexagram 03 · Line 5 · Wang Anshi’s Green Sprouts Loans — Funds May Not Reach Those in Need▶

九五｜Hexagram 03 · Line 5 · Wang Anshi’s Green Sprouts Loans — Funds May Not Reach Those in Need

017 case_id: XFIN-H03-L5 | grade: A | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Farmers' capital shortages led to official lending under the Green Sprouts Law, followed by disputes over distribution and repayment. The lesson is that funds may fail to reach those who need them. Compulsory allocation shows why increased lending does not necessarily mean widespread benefit. The Lesser Image says that withholding rich nourishment prevents its distribution from shining. Policy intentions and local implementation must be examined separately. Were loans voluntary, and could farmers repay principal and interest after harvest? This is a retrospective analogy, not historical use of the line.

Farm households needed funds before sowing or in agricultural slack periods, and private borrowing could be expensive. Wang Anshi promoted the Green Sprouts Law so officials would lend agricultural funds and later recover principal and interest. Local implementation was needed before money reached borrowers.

The scheme aimed to reduce dependence on high-interest private loans. But compulsory loan allocations, collection of principal and interest, and officials' performance assessments provoked controversy.

Debate concerned whether loans were voluntary, how interest was borne and whether officials pushed loans to meet targets. Even if the original aim was to relieve usury, coercion or repayment pressure could make borrowers' experience differ from the policy's promise. Studies describe the disputes but do not preserve complete loan rolls for every village.

The episode began with inadequate agricultural capital, moved to government lending and then to controversy over allocation and repayment. Results must be judged from borrowers' experience, not the policy name alone. The file cites QING on the Green Sprouts loan design and controversy from 1069, an agricultural-credit case rather than complete individual accounts.

019 Line H04 Meng Hexagram 04 · Line 1 · Rishengchang’s Draft Verification — Start With Clear Verification Rules▶

Source LINK confirms the implementation date and institutional framework, not each merchant’s response. The introduction of a rule and the process of rebuilding trust are distinct.

The case illustrates why durable agreements matter more than repeated short-term signals. Membership did not complete every adjustment.

The Han state expanded revenue measures, monopolies and procurement. These policies strengthened state capacity while affecting merchants and households.

The Book of Han describes both the mobilization of revenue and the pressures borne by society. Different policies did not impose identical burdens everywhere.

Military spending encouraged fiscal intervention, which in turn altered economic activity. The record does not provide a complete ledger for every family.

368 Line H62 Xiao Guo Hexagram 62 · Line 2 · ITRI Signs the RCA Technology-Transfer Agreement▶

九二｜Hexagram 62 · Line 2 · ITRI Signs the RCA Technology-Transfer Agreement

368 case_id: XFIN-H62-L2 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The RCA agreement illustrates learning within existing limits before independent implementation, not a prohibition on ambition.

Taiwan’s semiconductor ambitions required manufacturing know-how as well as equipment. Engineers needed practical process and management skills.

In 1976 ITRI signed a technology-transfer agreement with RCA and sent personnel to the United States for training.

Training did not complete the task. Returning engineers still had to adapt methods to local equipment, teams and suppliers.

The record identifies agreement, training and later implementation, not every trainee’s results or immediate commercial success.

369 Line H62 Xiao Guo Hexagram 62 · Line 3 · Rishengchang and Intercity Remittances▶

九三｜Hexagram 62 · Line 3 · Rishengchang and Intercity Remittances

369 case_id: XFIN-H62-L3 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Remittances required vigilance against forged drafts and errors; no particular attack is alleged.

Qing merchants needed to transfer funds between cities without transporting silver on dangerous journeys. They needed confidence that distant offices would honor payment instructions.

Rishengchang developed drafts and branch offices to make deposits in one location payable in another. The service reduced reliance on physical silver transport.

Authentication, accounts and trained employees allowed branches to check drafts. Expansion increased the need for consistent procedures and internal controls.

Institutional accounts describe the method but not every draft or holder. Trust depended on verification and payment, not simply the institution’s reputation.

370 Line H62 Xiao Guo Hexagram 62 · Line 4 · Beijing Banknotes Undergo Gradual Rehabilitation▶

370 case_id: XFIN-H62-L4 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The staged handling of Beijing notes resembles caution when immediate restoration is impossible.

After the 1916 suspension of redemption, holders of Beijing banknotes still faced uncertainty about settling their claims.

Bank of China histories describe a staged cleanup involving arrangements such as public bonds and deposit certificates.

Holders had to wait while institutions explained payment priorities and resources. The records do not establish identical outcomes for everyone.

The process extended over years. Restoring credit required performance at successive stages, not one new announcement.

371 Line H62 Xiao Guo Hexagram 62 · Line 5 · Wang Anshi’s Green Sprouts Loans Face Implementation Problems▶

九五｜Hexagram 62 · Line 5 · Wang Anshi’s Green Sprouts Loans Face Implementation Problems

371 case_id: XFIN-H62-L5 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The Green Sprouts policy shows how declared benefits can fail in implementation, without denying that some borrowers may have benefited.

Farmers often needed credit before harvest, when private loans could be expensive. The Green Sprouts policy proposed official agricultural lending.

The government supplied loans to be repaid with interest, but implementation depended on local officials and their incentives.

Disputes emerged over compulsory lending, repayment pressure and official targets. Outcomes could differ from the policy’s intended relief.

The sources preserve controversies rather than complete village-level ledgers. Results must be assessed beyond the declared purpose.

373 Line H63 Ji Ji Hexagram 63 · Line 1 · Hong Kong Launches the Tracker Fund after Market Intervention▶

初九｜Hexagram 63 · Line 1 · Hong Kong Launches the Tracker Fund after Market Intervention

373 case_id: XFIN-H63-L1 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Hong Kong’s staged disposal shows caution after extraordinary intervention; it does not endorse every trade.

During the Asian financial crisis, Hong Kong’s authorities bought equities in 1998. The resulting public holdings needed management and an eventual exit.

In November 1999 the Tracker Fund was launched to transfer part of those holdings gradually to market investors.

Orderly disposal required attention to market conditions. Launch did not mean every share was sold immediately or that all investors profited.

Official accounts distinguish intervention, portfolio management and a staged exit. Each was a different part of the policy.

375 Line H63 Ji Ji Hexagram 63 · Line 3 · Li Guangli’s Campaigns against Dayuan: Victory at a Cost▶

九三｜Hexagram 63 · Line 3 · Li Guangli’s Campaigns against Dayuan: Victory at a Cost

375 case_id: XFIN-H63-L3 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Li Guangli’s campaigns show that victory may entail exhaustion; the source says about four years, not three.

Emperor Wu of Han sent Li Guangli toward Dayuan after disputes involving envoys and the desire to obtain prized horses. The route created major logistical demands.

The first expedition failed amid inadequate supplies and resistance. A later, larger campaign forced Dayuan to negotiate and supply horses.

The Records of the Grand Historian describes severe losses and misconduct by officers. It says the two campaigns took roughly four years before the effort ended.

The achievement and the human cost must be weighed together. The source’s four-year chronology must not be rewritten as three years to fit the Lesser Image.

376 Line H63 Ji Ji Hexagram 63 · Line 4 · Taiwan’s Financial Reconstruction Fund and Bank Resolution▶

九四｜Hexagram 63 · Line 4 · Taiwan’s Financial Reconstruction Fund and Bank Resolution

376 case_id: XFIN-H63-L4 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

A bank-resolution framework requires vigilance after intervention; each institution’s outcome remains separate.

When financial institutions became distressed, depositors and businesses faced possible interruptions to payment and savings services.

Beginning in 2001, Taiwan’s Financial Reconstruction Fund supported arrangements for troubled institutions to be transferred or wound down.

Resolution still required asset assessment, funding and decisions on the transfer of obligations. The fund did not automatically erase losses.

Institutional documents describe the framework; outcomes for each bank require separate records. Continuity of services depended on execution.

377 Line H63 Ji Ji Hexagram 63 · Line 5 · Geng Shouchang Proposes Ever-Normal Granaries▶

九五｜Hexagram 63 · Line 5 · Geng Shouchang Proposes Ever-Normal Granaries

377 case_id: XFIN-H63-L5 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The granary proposal stresses timing and practical benefit rather than outward display; ritual language is metaphorical.

Grain prices varied with harvests and local supply. Farmers and consumers faced different pressures across the agricultural cycle.

Geng Shouchang proposed buying grain at lower prices and releasing stored supplies when prices rose.

Warehouses required storage, protection against losses and careful timing of sales. The principle did not guarantee ideal results in every year.

The sources explain the mechanism but do not preserve full annual accounts for each granary. Purchase, storage and release must be viewed together.

378 Line H63 Ji Ji Hexagram 63 · Line 6 · Early Han Treasury Reserves Rise and Are Later Drawn Down▶

上九｜Hexagram 63 · Line 6 · Early Han Treasury Reserves Rise and Are Later Drawn Down

378 case_id: XFIN-H63-L6 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Treasury wealth did not guarantee safety from later spending; present reserves need not last forever.

After conflict, early Han rulers restored agriculture and the tax base. Grain and money gradually accumulated in state stores.

The Book of Han describes periods of substantial reserves, followed by periods of heavier public expenditure.

Military and administrative commitments changed fiscal conditions. Accumulation and spending took place across different reigns.

A large reserve offered flexibility but not unlimited purchasing power. Historical accounts do not supply a complete annual budget.

380 Line H64 Wei Ji Hexagram 64 · Line 2 · ITRI’s Demonstration Fab Converts Training into Practice▶

The demonstration fab turned learning into disciplined practice before industrialization was complete.

381 Line H64 Wei Ji Hexagram 64 · Line 3 · The Gold Yuan Currency Reform Fails, 1948–1949▶

九三｜Hexagram 64 · Line 3 · The Gold Yuan Currency Reform Fails, 1948–1949

381 case_id: XFIN-H64-L3 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

The Gold Yuan failure illustrates reform before underlying conditions were secure, not a single-cause account of inflation.

In 1948 the Nationalist government introduced Gold Yuan notes amid civil war, fiscal strain and the depreciation of existing money.

The new currency did not halt inflation or restore purchasing power. Wartime financial and supply pressures continued after the formal reform.

The notes depreciated rapidly and ceased circulating in 1949. Holders suffered further erosion of purchasing power despite official assurances.

Historical collections establish the broad sequence but not the price of every good. A new currency name could not itself remove underlying economic constraints.

383 Line H64 Wei Ji Hexagram 64 · Line 5 · Bank of China Shanghai Resists the 1916 Redemption Suspension▶

九五｜Hexagram 64 · Line 5 · Bank of China Shanghai Resists the 1916 Redemption Suspension

383 case_id: XFIN-H64-L5 | grade: B | translation: V4.3.3 | schema: V4.3.4 | status: editorial_review

Actual redemption made a commitment credible; this is not unconditional praise of all banking policy.

In 1916 the Beiyang government ordered a suspension of redemption. Holders of notes and deposits faced uncertainty over the value of their claims.

The Bank of China’s Shanghai branch and some other offices resisted the order, seeking to preserve credit by continuing actual redemption.

Continuing payment required liquidity and operational decisions. The Shanghai experience cannot be assumed to describe every branch or noteholder.

Bank histories document the decision but not every customer account. A promise of payment mattered only when it could be fulfilled.
